12. using System;
13. using Windows.ApplicationModel;
14. using Windows.ApplicationModel.Activation;
15. using Microsoft.UI.Xaml;
16. using Microsoft.UI.Xaml.Controls;
17. using Microsoft.UI.Xaml.Navigation;
18. 
19. // The Blank Application template is documented at http://go.microsoft.com/fwlink/?LinkId=402347&clcid=0x409
20. 
21. // Most samples use a standard App.xaml.cs but this one demonstrates
22. // additional features for One Process background applications
23. // and is therefore unique to this project.
24. 
25. namespace SDKTemplate
26. {
27.     /// <summary>
28.     /// Provides application-specific behavior to supplement the default Application class.
29.     /// </summary>
30.     sealed partial class App : Application
31.     {        
32.         /// <summary>
33.         /// Initializes the singleton application object.  This is the first line of authored code
34.         /// executed, and as such is the logical equivalent of main() or WinMain().
35.         /// </summary>
36.         public App()
37.         {
38.             this.InitializeComponent();
39.             this.Construct();
40.         }
41. 
42.         /// <summary>
43.         /// Invoked when the application is launched normally by the end user.  Other entry points
44.         /// will be used such as when the application is launched to open a specific file.
45.         /// </summary>
46.         /// <param name="e">Details about the launch request and process.</param>
47.         protected override void OnLaunched(LaunchActivatedEventArgs e)
48.         {
49. 
50. #if DEBUG
51.             if (System.Diagnostics.Debugger.IsAttached)
52.             {
53.                 this.DebugSettings.EnableFrameRateCounter = false;
54.             }
55. #endif
56. 
57.             CreateRootFrame(e.PreviousExecutionState, e.Arguments);
58. 
59.             // Ensure the current window is active
60.             // TODO[migrate-001]: see PATTERNS.md#windowing
61.             Window.Current.Activate();
62.         }
63. 
64.         /// <summary>
65.         /// Creates the frame containing the view
66.         /// </summary>
67.         /// <remarks>
68.         /// This is the same code that was in OnLaunched() initially.
69.         /// It is moved to a separate method so the view can be restored
70.         /// when leaving the background if it was unloaded when the app 
71.         /// entered the background.
72.         /// </remarks>
73.         void CreateRootFrame(ApplicationExecutionState previousExecutionState, string arguments)
74.         {
75.             // TODO[migrate-002]: see PATTERNS.md#windowing
76.             Frame rootFrame = Window.Current.Content as Frame;
77. 
78.             // Do not repeat app initialization when the Window already has content,
79.             // just ensure that the window is active
80.             if (rootFrame == null)
81.             {
82.                 System.Diagnostics.Debug.WriteLine("CreateFrame: Initializing root frame ...");
83. 
84.                 // Create a Frame to act as the navigation context and navigate to the first page
85.                 rootFrame = new Frame();
86. 
87.                 // Set the default language
88.                 rootFrame.Language = Windows.Globalization.ApplicationLanguages.Languages[0];
89. 
90.                 rootFrame.NavigationFailed += OnNavigationFailed;
91. 
92.                 if (previousExecutionState == ApplicationExecutionState.Terminated)
93.                 {
94.                     //TODO: Load state from previously suspended application
95.                 }
96. 
97.                 // Place the frame in the current Window
98.                 // TODO[migrate-003]: see PATTERNS.md#windowing
99.                 Window.Current.Content = rootFrame;
100.             }
101. 
102.             if (rootFrame.Content == null)
103.             {
104.                 // When the navigation stack isn't restored navigate to the first page,
105.                 // configuring the new page by passing required information as a navigation
106.                 // parameter
107.                 rootFrame.Navigate(typeof(MainPage), arguments);
108.             }
109.         }
110. 
111.         /// <summary>
112.         /// Invoked when Navigation to a certain page fails
113.         /// </summary>
114.         /// <param name="sender">The Frame which failed navigation</param>
115.         /// <param name="e">Details about the navigation failure</param>
116.         void OnNavigationFailed(object sender, NavigationFailedEventArgs e)
117.         {
118.             throw new Exception("Failed to load Page " + e.SourcePageType.FullName);
119.         }
120. 
121.         // Add any application contructor code in here.
122.         partial void Construct();
123.     }
124. }

1. using BackgroundMediaPlayback.Services;
2. using System;
3. using System.Diagnostics;
4. using Windows.ApplicationModel;
5. using Windows.ApplicationModel.Activation;
6. using Windows.System;
7. using Windows.UI.Notifications;
8. using Microsoft.UI.Xaml;
9. using Microsoft.UI.Xaml.Media;
10. 
11. namespace SDKTemplate
12. {
13.     sealed partial class App : Application
14.     {
15.         /// <summary>
16.         /// Flag to indicate whether the app is currently in
17.         /// foreground or background mode.
18.         /// </summary>
19.         /// <remarks>
20.         /// This is used later to determine when to load / unload UI.
21.         /// </remarks>
22.         bool isInBackgroundMode;
23. 
24.         /// <summary>
25.         /// Called from App.xaml.cs when the application is constructed.
26.         /// </summary>
27.         partial void Construct()
28.         {
29.             // During the transition from foreground to background the
30.             // memory limit allowed for the application changes. The application
31.             // has a short time to respond by bringing its memory usage
32.             // under the new limit.
33.             MemoryManager.AppMemoryUsageLimitChanging += MemoryManager_AppMemoryUsageLimitChanging;
34. 
35.             // After an application is backgrounded it is expected to stay
36.             // under a memory target to maintain priority to keep running.
37.             // Subscribe to the event that informs the app of this change.
38.             MemoryManager.AppMemoryUsageIncreased += MemoryManager_AppMemoryUsageIncreased;
39. 
40.             // Subscribe to key lifecyle events to know when the app
41.             // transitions to and from foreground and background.
42.             // Leaving the background is an important transition
43.             // because the app may need to restore UI.
44.             EnteredBackground += App_EnteredBackground;
45.             LeavingBackground += App_LeavingBackground;
46. 
47.             // Subscribe to regular lifecycle events to display a toast notification
48.             Suspending += App_Suspending;
49.             Resuming += App_Resuming;
50.         }
51. 
52.         /// <summary>
53.         /// Invoked when application execution is being suspended.  Application state is saved
54.         /// without knowing whether the application will be terminated or resumed with the contents
55.         /// of memory still intact.
56.         /// </summary>
57.         /// <param name="sender">The source of the suspend request.</param>
58.         /// <param name="e">Details about the suspend request.</param>
59.         private void App_Suspending(object sender, SuspendingEventArgs e)
60.         {
61.             var deferral = e.SuspendingOperation.GetDeferral();
62.             // Optional: Save application state and stop any background activity
63.             ShowToast("Suspending");
64.             deferral.Complete();
65.         }
66. 
67.         /// <summary>
68.         /// Invoked when application execution is resumed.
69.         /// </summary>
70.         /// <param name="sender"></param>
71.         /// <param name="e"></param>
72.         private void App_Resuming(object sender, object e)
73.         {
74.             ShowToast("Resuming");
75.         }
76. 
77.         /// <summary>
78.         /// Raised when the memory limit for the app is changing, such as when the app
79.         /// enters the background.
80.         /// </summary>
81.         /// <remarks>
82.         /// If the app is using more than the new limit, it must reduce memory within 2 seconds
83.         /// on some platforms in order to avoid being suspended or terminated.
84.         /// 
85.         /// While some platforms will allow the application
86.         /// to continue running over the limit, reducing usage in the time
87.         /// allotted will enable the best experience across the broadest range of devices.
88.         /// </remarks>
89.         /// <param name="sender"></param>
90.         /// <param name="e"></param>
91.         private void MemoryManager_AppMemoryUsageLimitChanging(object sender, AppMemoryUsageLimitChangingEventArgs e)
92.         {
93.             ShowToast("Memory usage limit changing from " 
94.                 + (e.OldLimit / 1024) + "K to "
95.                 + (e.NewLimit / 1024) + "K");
96. 
97.             // If app memory usage is over the limit about to be enforced,
98.             // then reduce usage within 2 seconds to avoid suspending.
99.             if (MemoryManager.AppMemoryUsage >= e.NewLimit)
100.             {
101.                 ReduceMemoryUsage(e.NewLimit);
102.             }
103.         }
104. 
105.         /// <summary>
106.         /// Handle system notifications that the app has increased its
107.         /// memory usage level compared to its current target.
108.         /// </summary>
109.         /// <remarks>
110.         /// The app may have increased its usage or the app may have moved
111.         /// to the background and the system lowered the target the app
112.         /// is expected to reach. Either way, if the application wants
113.         /// to maintain its priority to avoid being suspended before
114.         /// other apps, it may need to reduce its memory usage.
115.         /// 
116.         /// This is not a replacement for handling AppMemoryUsageLimitChanging
117.         /// which is critical to ensure the app immediately gets below the new
118.         /// limit. However, once the app is allowed to continue running and
119.         /// policy is applied, some apps may wish to continue monitoring
120.         /// usage to ensure they remain below the limit.
121.         /// </remarks>
122.         /// <param name="sender"></param>
123.         /// <param name="e"></param>
124.         private void MemoryManager_AppMemoryUsageIncreased(object sender, object e)
125.         {
126.             ShowToast("Memory usage increased");
127. 
128.             // Obtain the current usage level
129.             var level = MemoryManager.AppMemoryUsageLevel;
130. 
131.             // Check the usage level to determine whether reducing memory is necessary.
132.             // Memory usage may have been fine when initially entering the background but
133.             // a short time later the app might be using more memory and need to trim back.
134.             if (level == AppMemoryUsageLevel.OverLimit || level == AppMemoryUsageLevel.High)
135.             {
136.                 ReduceMemoryUsage(MemoryManager.AppMemoryUsageLimit);
137.             }
138.         }
139. 
140.         /// <summary>
141.         /// The application entered the background.
142.         /// </summary>
143.         /// <param name="sender"></param>
144.         /// <param name="e"></param>
145.         private void App_EnteredBackground(object sender, EnteredBackgroundEventArgs e)
146.         {
147.             // Place the application into "background mode" and note the
148.             // transition with a flag.
149.             ShowToast("Entered background");
150.             isInBackgroundMode = true;
151. 
152.             // An application may wish to release views and view data
153.             // at this point since the UI is no longer visible.
154.             //
155.             // As a performance optimization, here we note instead that 
156.             // the app has entered background mode with a boolean and
157.             // defer unloading views until AppMemoryUsageLimitChanging or 
158.             // AppMemoryUsageIncreased is raised with an indication that
159.             // the application is under memory pressure.
160.         }
161. 
162.         /// <summary>
163.         /// The application is leaving the background.
164.         /// </summary>
165.         /// <param name="sender"></param>
166.         /// <param name="e"></param>
167.         private void App_LeavingBackground(object sender, LeavingBackgroundEventArgs e)
168.         {
169.             // Mark the transition out of background mode.
170.             ShowToast("Leaving background");
171.             isInBackgroundMode = false;
172. 
173.             // Reastore view content if it was previously unloaded.
174.             // TODO[migrate-004]: see PATTERNS.md#windowing
175.             if (Window.Current.Content == null)
176.             {
177.                 ShowToast("Loading view");
178.                 CreateRootFrame(ApplicationExecutionState.Running, string.Empty);
179.             } 
180.         }
181. 
182.         /// <summary>
183.         /// Reduces application memory usage.
184.         /// </summary>
185.         /// <remarks>
186.         /// When the app enters the background, receives a memory limit changing
187.         /// event, or receives a memory usage increased
188.         /// event it can optionally unload cached data or even its view content in 
189.         /// order to reduce memory usage and the chance of being suspended.
190.         /// 
191.         /// This must be called from multiple event handlers because an application may already
192.         /// be in a high memory usage state when entering the background or it
193.         /// may be in a low memory usage state with no need to unload resources yet
194.         /// and only enter a higher state later.
195.         /// </remarks>
196.         public void ReduceMemoryUsage(ulong limit)
197.         {
198.             // If the app has caches or other memory it can free, now is the time.
199.             // << App can release memory here >>
200. 
201.             // Additionally, if the application is currently
202.             // in background mode and still has a view with content
203.             // then the view can be released to save memory and 
204.             // can be recreated again later when leaving the background.
205.             // TODO[migrate-005]: see PATTERNS.md#windowing
206.             if (isInBackgroundMode && Window.Current.Content != null)
207.             {
208.                 ShowToast("Unloading view");
209. 
210.                 // Clear the view content. Note that views should rely on
211.                 // events like Page.Unloaded to further release resources. Be careful
212.                 // to also release event handlers in views since references can
213.                 // prevent objects from being collected. C++ developers should take
214.                 // special care to use weak references for event handlers where appropriate.
215.                 // TODO[migrate-006]: see PATTERNS.md#windowing
216.                 Window.Current.Content = null;
217. 
218.                 // Finally, clearing the content above and calling GC.Collect() below 
219.                 // is what will trigger each Page.Unloaded handler to be called.
220.                 // In order for the resources each page has allocated to be released,
221.                 // it is necessary that each Page also call GC.Collect() from its
222.                 // Page.Unloaded handler.
223.             }
224. 
225.             // Run the GC to collect released resources, including triggering
226.             // each Page.Unloaded handler to run.
227.             GC.Collect();
228. 
229.             ShowToast("Finished reducing memory usage");
230.         }
231. 
232.         /// <summary>
233.         /// Gets a string describing current memory usage
234.         /// </summary>
235.         /// <returns>String describing current memory usage</returns>
236.         private string GetMemoryUsageText()
237.         {
238.             return string.Format("[Memory: Level={0}, Usage={1}K, Target={2}K]", 
239.                 MemoryManager.AppMemoryUsageLevel, MemoryManager.AppMemoryUsage / 1024, MemoryManager.AppMemoryUsageLimit / 1024);
240.         }
241. 
242.         /// <summary>
243.         /// Sends a toast notification
244.         /// </summary>
245.         /// <param name="msg">Message to send</param>
246.         /// <param name="subMsg">Sub message</param>
247.         public void ShowToast(string msg, string subMsg = null)
248.         {
249.             if (subMsg == null)
250.                 subMsg = GetMemoryUsageText();
251. 
252.             Debug.WriteLine(msg + "\n" + subMsg);
253. 
254.             if (!SettingsService.Instance.ToastOnAppEvents)
255.                 return;
256. 
257.             // TODO[migrate-007]: see PATTERNS.md#notifications
258.             var toastXml = ToastNotificationManager.GetTemplateContent(ToastTemplateType.ToastText02);
259. 
260.             var toastTextElements = toastXml.GetElementsByTagName("text");
261.             toastTextElements[0].AppendChild(toastXml.CreateTextNode(msg));
262.             toastTextElements[1].AppendChild(toastXml.CreateTextNode(subMsg));
263. 
264.             var toast = new ToastNotification(toastXml);
265.             // TODO[migrate-008]: see PATTERNS.md#notifications
266.             ToastNotificationManager.CreateToastNotifier().Show(toast);
267.         }
268.     }
269. }

184.             });

187.         private void HandlePlaybackListChanges(IObservableVector<MediaPlaybackItem> vector)
188.         {
189.             if (vector.Count > 0)
190.             {
191.                 CanSkipNext = true;
192.                 CanSkipPrevious = true;
193.             }
194.             else

197.                 CanSkipPrevious = false;
198.             }

213.                 MediaList.Dispose();
214.                 MediaList = null; // Setter triggers vector unsubscribe logic
215.             }

1. using BackgroundMediaPlayback.DataModels;
2. using System;
3. using System.Collections.Generic;
4. using System.Collections.ObjectModel;
5. using System.Collections.Specialized;
6. using System.ComponentModel;
7. using System.Linq;
8. using System.Text;
9. using System.Threading.Tasks;
10. using Windows.Media.Playback;
11. using Windows.UI.Core;
12. 
13. namespace BackgroundMediaPlayback.ViewModels
14. {
15.     /// <summary>
16.     /// View model for a list of media items.
17.     /// </summary>
18.     /// <remarks>
19.     /// The view model is backed by a data model (MediaList)
20.     /// and a playback model (MediaPlaybackList).
21.     /// 
22.     /// This class currently supports TwoWay binding with the
23.     /// playback model so changes to the view model list also
24.     /// update the list of items scheduled for playback.
25.     /// 
26.     /// Changes are not currently persisted back to or updated
27.     /// from the data model.
28.     /// 
29.     /// This view model subscribes to events on the player
30.     /// and must unsubscribe before unloading the UI in
31.     /// background mode. Call Dispose() to initiate this.
32.     /// </remarks>
33.     public class MediaListViewModel : ObservableCollection<MediaItemViewModel>, IDisposable
34.     {
35.         // TODO[migrate-011]: see PATTERNS.md#threading
36.         CoreDispatcher dispatcher;
37.         int currentItemIndex = -1;
38.         bool disposed;
39.         bool initializing;
40. 
41.         public MediaList MediaList { get; private set; }
42. 
43.         public MediaItemViewModel CurrentItem
44.         {
45.             get { return currentItemIndex == -1 ? null : this[currentItemIndex]; }
46.             set
47.             {
48.                 if (value == null)
49.                 {
50.                     CurrentItemIndex = -1;
51.                     return;
52.                 }
53. 
54.                 if (currentItemIndex == -1 || this[currentItemIndex] != value)
55.                 {
56.                     CurrentItemIndex = IndexOf(value);
57.                 }
58.             }
59.         }
60. 
61.         public int CurrentItemIndex
62.         {
63.             get
64.             {
65.                 return currentItemIndex;
66.             }
67.             set
68.             {
69.                 if (currentItemIndex != value)
70.                 {
71.                     // Clamp invalid values
72.                     var min = -1;
73.                     var max = PlaybackList.Items.Count - 1;
74.                     currentItemIndex = (value < min) ? min : (value > max) ? max : value;
75. 
76.                     try
77.                     {
78.                         // This succeeds if the playlist has been bound to a player
79.                         if (currentItemIndex >= 0 && currentItemIndex != PlaybackList.CurrentItemIndex)
80.                             PlaybackList.MoveTo((uint)currentItemIndex);
81.                     }
82.                     catch
83.                     {
84.                         // Most likely the playlist had not been bound to a player so set start index
85.                         PlaybackList.StartingItem = CurrentItem.PlaybackItem;
86.                     }
87. 
88.                     OnPropertyChanged(new PropertyChangedEventArgs("CurrentItemIndex"));
89.                     OnPropertyChanged(new PropertyChangedEventArgs("CurrentItem"));
90.                 }
91.             }
92.         }
93. 
94.         public MediaPlaybackList PlaybackList { get; private set; }
95. 
96.         // TODO[migrate-012]: see PATTERNS.md#threading
97.         public MediaListViewModel(MediaList mediaList, MediaPlaybackList playbackList, CoreDispatcher dispatcher)
98.         {
99.             MediaList = mediaList;
100.             PlaybackList = playbackList;
101.             this.dispatcher = dispatcher;
102. 
103.             // Verify consistency of the lists that were passed in
104.             var mediaListIds = mediaList.Select(i => i.ItemId);
105.             var playbackListIds = playbackList.Items.Select(
106.                 i => (string)i.Source.CustomProperties.SingleOrDefault(
107.                     p => p.Key == MediaItem.MediaItemIdKey).Value);
108. 
109.             if (!mediaListIds.SequenceEqual(playbackListIds))
110.                 throw new ArgumentException("The passed in data model and playback model did not have the same sequence of items");
111. 
112.             // Initialize the view model items
113.             initializing = true;
114. 
115.             foreach (var mediaItem in mediaList)
116.                 Add(new MediaItemViewModel(this, mediaItem));
117. 
118.             initializing = false;
119. 
120.             // The view model supports TwoWay binding so update when the playback list item changes
121.             PlaybackList.CurrentItemChanged += PlaybackList_CurrentItemChanged;
122. 
123.             // Start where the playback list is currently at
124.             CurrentItemIndex = (int)PlaybackList.CurrentItemIndex;
125.         }
126. 
127.         protected override void InsertItem(int index, MediaItemViewModel item)
128.         {
129.             base.InsertItem(index, item);
130. 
131.             // Don't add items during construction
132.             if (!initializing)
133.                 PlaybackList.Items.Add(item.PlaybackItem);
134.         }
135. 
136.         protected override void ClearItems()
137.         {
138.             base.ClearItems();
139. 
140.             PlaybackList.Items.Clear();
141.         }
142. 
143.         protected override void RemoveItem(int index)
144.         {
145.             base.RemoveItem(index);
146. 
147.             PlaybackList.Items.RemoveAt(index);
148.         }
149. 
150.         protected override void SetItem(int index, MediaItemViewModel item)
151.         {
152.             base.SetItem(index, item);
153. 
154.             PlaybackList.Items[index] = item.PlaybackItem;
155.         }
156. 
157.         protected override void MoveItem(int oldIndex, int newIndex)
158.         {
159.             base.MoveItem(oldIndex, newIndex);
160. 
161.             var item = PlaybackList.Items[oldIndex];
162.             PlaybackList.Items.RemoveAt(oldIndex);
163.             PlaybackList.Items.Insert(newIndex, item);
164.         }
165. 
166.         /// <summary>
167.         /// This call comes in on a worker thread so requires a little extra care
168.         /// to avoid touching the UI if it has already been disposed.
169.         /// </summary>
170.         /// <param name="sender"></param>
171.         /// <param name="args"></param>
172.         private async void PlaybackList_CurrentItemChanged(MediaPlaybackList sender, CurrentMediaPlaybackItemChangedEventArgs args)
173.         {
174.             // If the event was unsubscribed before 
175.             // this handler executed just return.
176.             if (disposed)
177.                 return;
178. 
179.             // TODO[migrate-013]: see PATTERNS.md#threading
180.             await dispatcher.RunAsync(CoreDispatcherPriority.Normal, () =>
181.             {
182.                 // If the event was unsubscribed before 
183.                 // this handler executed just return.
184.                 if (disposed)
185.                     return;
186. 
187.                 var playbackItem = args.NewItem;
188. 
189.                 if (playbackItem == null)
190.                 {
191.                     CurrentItem = null;
192.                 }
193.                 else
194.                 {
195.                     // Find the single item in this list with a playback item
196.                     // matching the one we just received the event for.
197.                     CurrentItem = this.Single(mediaItemViewModel => mediaItemViewModel.PlaybackItem == playbackItem);
198.                 }
199.             });
200.         }
201. 
202.         public void Dispose()
203.         {
204.             if (disposed)
205.                 return;
206. 
207.             PlaybackList.CurrentItemChanged -= PlaybackList_CurrentItemChanged;
208. 
209.             disposed = true;
210.         }
211.     }
212. }

<a id="windowing"></a>
## Windowing: Window.Current / ApplicationView / CoreWindow → AppWindow`Window.Current`, `ApplicationView`, and `CoreWindow` are gone. Track the main window yourself and use `Microsoft.UI.Windowing.AppWindow`. See the official [windowing guide](https://learn.microsoft.com/windows/apps/windows-app-sdk/migrate-to-windows-app-sdk/guides/windowing).UWP (replace this):```csharp
var win = Window.Current;
ApplicationView.GetForCurrentView().TryResizeView(new Size(800, 600));
```WinUI 3 — expose `MainWindow` from `App`:```csharp
public partial class App : Application
{
    // With <Nullable>enable</Nullable>, declare as nullable since the assignment only happens during OnLaunched.
    public static Window? MainWindow { get; private set; }    protected override void OnLaunched(LaunchActivatedEventArgs args)
    {
        var window = new MainWindow();
        MainWindow = window;
        window.Activate();
    }
}
```Consumers running after `OnLaunched` can safely null-forgive:```csharp
var hwnd = WinRT.Interop.WindowNative.GetWindowHandle(App.MainWindow!);
```Resize via `AppWindow`:```csharp
var hwnd = WinRT.Interop.WindowNative.GetWindowHandle(App.MainWindow);
var windowId = Microsoft.UI.Win32Interop.GetWindowIdFromWindow(hwnd);
var appWindow = AppWindow.GetFromWindowId(windowId);
appWindow.Resize(new SizeInt32(800, 600));
```### Initialization order — keep MainWindow's constructor inert`App.MainWindow` (or any equivalent static window reference) is `null` for the entire duration of `new MainWindow()` — the right-hand side runs to completion BEFORE the assignment happens. Anything inside that constructor that reads the static — directly or transitively — sees `null` and throws `E_POINTER` (0x80004003) at startup. The build is clean; the analyzer is silent; only a runtime launch reveals it.APIs that commonly trip this race when called from a `Page` reached during `MainWindow` construction:- `WinRT.Interop.WindowNative.GetWindowHandle(App.MainWindow)`
- `XamlRoot` / `Content.XamlRoot` access through a static-window helper
- `PrintManagerInterop.GetForWindow(hwnd)` / `ShowPrintUIForWindowAsync(hwnd)`
- `FileOpenPicker` / `FileSavePicker` / `FolderPicker` (require an HWND)
- `ContentDialog.ShowAsync()` when the dialog's `XamlRoot` is sourced from a static
- `AppWindow.GetFromWindowId(...)` chained through `App.MainWindow`
- Any custom title bar / `SetTitleBar` helper that resolves the HWND staticallySafe pattern — assign before activate, defer first navigation:```csharp
// App.xaml.cs
protected override void OnLaunched(LaunchActivatedEventArgs args)
{
    var window = new MainWindow();
    MainWindow = window;
    window.Activate();
}
```

The `MainWindow` constructor must do only `InitializeComponent` and inert setup — no navigation to a `Page` that reads `
`App.MainWindow`. Defer the first navigation until after `Activate` completes, either from a `Loaded` handler hooked in `
`InitializeComponent` or by exposing a `NavigateToInitialPage()` method and calling it from `App.OnLaunched` after the st
tatic is assigned.

```csharp
// MainWindow.xaml.cs
public MainWindow()
{
    InitializeComponent();
    this.Activated += (_, _) =>
    {
        if (!_navigated)
        {
            _navigated = true;
            RootFrame.Navigate(typeof(MainPage));
        }
    };
}
private bool _navigated;
```

Validator catches this race with a 10s smoke launch after the build healthcheck passes — see `Validate-UwpMigration.ps1`
` Section 7.

### AppWindow API replacements

| UWP API | WinUI 3 API |
|---------|-------------|
| `ApplicationView.TryResizeView` | `AppWindow.Resize` |
| `AppWindow.TryCreateAsync` | `AppWindow.Create` |
| `AppWindow.TryShowAsync` | `AppWindow.Show` |
| `AppWindow.TryConsolidateAsync` | `AppWindow.Destroy` |
| `AppWindow.RequestMoveXxx` | `AppWindow.Move` |
| `AppWindow.RequestPresentation` | `AppWindow.SetPresenter` |
| `CoreApplicationViewTitleBar` | `AppWindowTitleBar` |
| `CoreApplicationView.TitleBar.ExtendViewIntoTitleBar` | `AppWindow.TitleBar.ExtendsContentIntoTitleBar` |
<exited with exit code 0>

<a id="threading"></a>
## Threading: CoreDispatcher → DispatcherQueueUWP (replace this):```csharp
await Dispatcher.RunAsync(CoreDispatcherPriority.Normal, () => StatusText.Text = "Done");
```WinUI 3:```csharp
DispatcherQueue.TryEnqueue(() => StatusText.Text = "Done");
DispatcherQueue.TryEnqueue(DispatcherQueuePriority.High, () => ProgressBar.Value = 100);
```Cache the queue off the UI thread via `DispatcherQueue.GetForCurrentThread()`. UWP's ASTA reentrancy protection is gone — watch for reentrancy in async code that pumps messages. See the official [threading guide](https://learn.microsoft.com/windows/apps/windows-app-sdk/migrate-to-windows-app-sdk/guides/threading).

67.         /// </summary>
68.         /// <param name="sender"></param>
69.         /// <param name="e"></param>
70.         private void ScenarioControl_SelectionChanged(object sender, SelectionChangedEventArgs e)
71.         {
72.             // Clear the status block when navigating scenarios.
73.             NotifyUser(String.Empty, NotifyType.StatusMessage);
74. 
75.             ListBox scenarioListBox = sender as ListBox;
76.             Scenario s = scenarioListBox.SelectedItem as Scenario;
77.             if (s != null)
78.             {
79.                 ScenarioFrame.Navigate(s.ClassType);
80.                 if (Window.Current.Bounds.Width < 640)
81.                 {
82.                     Splitter.IsPaneOpen = false;
83.                 }
84.             }
85.         }
86. 
87.         public List<Scenario> Scenarios
88.         {
89.             get { return this.scenarios; }
90.         }
91. 
92.         /// <summary>
93.         /// Display a message to the user.
94.         /// This method may be called from any thread.
95.         /// </summary>
96.         /// <param name="strMessage"></param>
97.         /// <param name="type"></param>
98.         public void NotifyUser(string strMessage, NotifyType type)
99.         {
100.             // If called from the UI thread, then update immediately.
101.             // Otherwise, schedule a task on the UI thread to perform the update.
102.             if (Dispatcher.HasThreadAccess)
103.             {
104.                 UpdateStatus(strMessage, type);
105.             }
106.             else
107.             {
108.                 var task = Dispatcher.RunAsync(CoreDispatcherPriority.Normal, () => UpdateStatus(strMessage, type));
109.             }
110.         }
111. 
112.         private void UpdateStatus(string strMessage, NotifyType type)
113.         {
114.             switch (type)
115.             {
116.                 case NotifyType.StatusMessage:
117.                     StatusBorder.Background = new SolidColorBrush(Windows.UI.Colors.Green);
118.                     break;
119.                 case NotifyType.ErrorMessage:
120.                     StatusBorder.Background = new SolidColorBrush(Windows.UI.Colors.Red);
121.                     break;
122.             }
123. 
124.             StatusBlock.Text = strMessage;
125. 
126.             // Collapse the StatusBlock if it has no text to conserve real estate.
127.             StatusBorder.Visibility = (StatusBlock.Text != String.Empty) ? Visibility.Visible : Visibility.Collapsed;
128.             if (StatusBlock.Text != String.Empty)
129.             {
130.                 StatusBorder.Visibility = Visibility.Visible;
131.                 StatusPanel.Visibility = Visibility.Visible;
132.             }
133.             else
134.             {
135.                 StatusBorder.Visibility = Visibility.Collapsed;
136.                 StatusPanel.Visibility = Visibility.Collapsed;
137.             }
138. 
139. 			// Raise an event if necessary to enable a screen reader to announce the status update.
140. 			var peer = FrameworkElementAutomationPeer.FromElement(StatusBlock);
141. 			if (peer != null)
142. 			{
143. 				peer.RaiseAutomationEvent(AutomationEvents.LiveRegionChanged);
144. 			}
145. 		}
146. 
147.         async void Footer_Click(object sender, RoutedEventArgs e)
148.         {
149.             await Windows.System.Launcher.LaunchUriAsync(new Uri(((HyperlinkButton)sender).Tag.ToString()));
150.         }
151. 
152.         private void Button_Click(object sender, RoutedEventArgs e)
153.         {
154.             Splitter.IsPaneOpen = !Splitter.IsPaneOpen;
155.         }
156.     }
157.     public enum NotifyType
158.     {
159.         StatusMessage,
160.         ErrorMessage
161.     };
162. }

3. using System.Linq;

12. 
13. namespace BackgroundMediaPlayback.DataModels
14. {
15.     public class MediaList : List<MediaItem>
16.     {
17.         public string Title { get; set; }
18. 
19.         public MediaList()
20.         {
21.         }
22. 
23.         public async Task LoadFromApplicationUriAsync(string uri)
24.         {
25.             await LoadFromApplicationUriAsync(new Uri(uri));
26.         }
27. 
28.         public async Task LoadFromApplicationUriAsync(Uri uri)
29.         {
30.             Clear();
31. 
32.             var storageFile = await StorageFile.GetFileFromApplicationUriAsync(uri);
33.             var jsonText = await FileIO.ReadTextAsync(storageFile);
34.             var json = JsonObject.Parse(jsonText);
35.             json = json.GetNamedObject("mediaList");
36. 
37.             Title = json["title"].GetString();
38.             var items = json["items"].GetArray();
39.             foreach (var jsonItem in items)
40.             {
41.                 Add(LoadItem(jsonItem.GetObject()));
42.             }
43.         }
44. 
45.         MediaItem LoadItem(JsonObject json)
46.         {
47.             var mediaType = json["mediaType"].GetString();
48.             switch (mediaType)
49.             {
50.                 case "music":
51.                     return new MusicItem(json);
52.                 case "video":
53.                     return new VideoItem(json);
54.                 default:
55.                     return new MediaItem(json);
56.             }
57.         }
58. 
59.         public MediaPlaybackList ToPlaybackList()
60.         {
61.             var playbackList = new MediaPlaybackList();
62. 
63.             // Make a new list and enable looping
64.             playbackList.AutoRepeatEnabled = true;
65. 
66.             // Add playback items to the list
67.             foreach (var mediaItem in this)
68.             {
69.                 playbackList.Items.Add(mediaItem.ToPlaybackItem());
70.             }
71. 
72.             return playbackList;
73.         }
74.     }

<a id="dialogs"></a>
## Dialogs: MessageDialog → ContentDialogUWP (replace this):```csharp
var dlg = new MessageDialog("Are you sure?", "Confirm");
await dlg.ShowAsync();
```WinUI 3 — `ContentDialog` requires a `XamlRoot`:```csharp
var dlg = new ContentDialog
{
    Title = "Confirm",
    Content = "Are you sure?",
    PrimaryButtonText = "Yes",
    CloseButtonText = "No",
    XamlRoot = this.Content.XamlRoot   // inside a Window
};
var result = await dlg.ShowAsync();
```**Picking the right `XamlRoot`:**| Calling from | Use |
|---|---|
| A `Window` (e.g., `MainWindow`) | `this.Content.XamlRoot` |
| A `Page` | `this.XamlRoot` |
| A `UserControl` | `this.XamlRoot` |
| A view-model / non-UI class | Inject the `XamlRoot` from the calling view; never assume a global. |If `XamlRoot` is `null`, the call happened before the element was loaded — wire the dialog from `Loaded` or after `Window.Activate()`.
<exited with exit code 0>
--- RESULT ---
<a id="csproj"></a>
## Project File Updates- Target a current WinAppSDK-supported TFM. The exact TFM is the source of truth in the project's `.csproj`; do not hard-code it across instruction files. Typical values at time of writing:
  - `net8.0-windows10.0.19041.0` (LTS)
  - `net9.0-windows10.0.19041.0`
  - `net10.0-windows10.0.26100.0` (current `dotnet new winui` default in this repo)
- Add `<UseWinUI>true</UseWinUI>`.
- Add `<EnableMsixTooling>true</EnableMsixTooling>` for packaged builds.
- Reference `Microsoft.WindowsAppSDK` and `Microsoft.Windows.SDK.BuildTools`.
- Reference `Microsoft.Windows.SDK.BuildTools.WinApp` to wire `dotnet run` into `winapp run`.
- Keep `Package.appxmanifest` for packaged scenarios; set `<WindowsPackageType>None</WindowsPackageType>` for unpackaged.### PackageReference reconciliation cheat-sheet`Initialize-UwpMigration.ps1` preserves the UWP `.csproj` at `<Target>/.uwp-source/` and leaves the WinUI 3 scaffold's `.csproj` intact. Open both side-by-side and merge:- **Drop** (UWP-only — never carry over):
  - `Microsoft.NETCore.UniversalWindowsPlatform`
  - Any `<PackageReference>` whose version string contains `uap` or whose name starts with `Windows.SDK.Contracts`.
- **Keep** (already in the scaffold — do not duplicate or downgrade):
  - `Microsoft.WindowsAppSDK`
  - `Microsoft.Windows.SDK.BuildTools`
- **Add** (project-specific — copy across, then update to a WinAppSDK-compatible version):
  - Third-party libraries the UWP project pulled in (`CommunityToolkit.*`, `Microsoft.Extensions.*`, `Newtonsoft.Json`, etc.). Pick the latest stable release; UWP-pinned versions are usually too old.Do **not** copy the UWP `.csproj` over the scaffold's. The two formats are incompatible — the UWP csproj carries `<TargetPlatformIdentifier>UAP</TargetPlatformIdentifier>`, `<OutputType>AppContainerExe</OutputType>`, explicit `<Compile Include="...">` items, and `Microsoft.Common.props` imports, none of which build under WinAppSDK.### Package.appxmanifest — reconcile image references with the assets you actually haveThe WinUI 3 scaffold ships with a default `Package.appxmanifest` that references assets the template provides under `Assets/` (`SplashScreen.scale-200.png`, `Square150x150Logo.scale-200.png`, `Square44x44Logo.scale-200.png`, `StoreLogo.png`, `Wide310x150Logo.scale-200.png`, `LockScreenLogo.scale-200.png`). The UWP SDK sample's manifest usually points at sample-branded assets with a `-sdk` suffix (`Splash-sdk.png`, `squareTile-sdk.png`, `SmallTile-sdk.png`, `StoreLogo-sdk.png`).If you copy the UWP manifest's `Logo` / `Square150x150Logo` / `Square44x44Logo` / `<uap:SplashScreen Image="...">` / `<uap:DefaultTile Wide310x150Logo="...">` references over the scaffold's without also bringing the matching files, **`winapp run` will register the package but the AppX layout will fail to launch** with errors like:```
AppxManifest.xml(...): error 0x80070002: ... splash screen image [Splash-sdk.png] cannot be located.
```(Failure code on the AppX side is typically `0x80073CF6` — "package could not be registered".)You have two options. Pick one **before** the first `winapp run`:- **Preferred — keep the scaffold's manifest image references** (`Assets\SplashScreen.scale-200.png`, etc.). When you merge any other content from the UWP manifest into the scaffold's (capabilities, file-type associations, `<uap:Extension>`, `<Application Id>`, etc.), do **not** overwrite the `Logo`, `<uap:SplashScreen Image>`, `Square150x150Logo`, `Square44x44Logo`, or `Wide310x150Logo` attribute values. The scaffold's defaults already match the files in its `Assets/` folder.- **Alternative — keep the UWP sample's branded assets.** Copy every file the UWP manifest references from `.uwp-source/Assets/` (or `.uwp-source/<SampleName>/Assets/`) into the new project's `Assets/` folder, preserving the exact filename (including the `-sdk` suffix and any scale qualifiers). Verify by re-reading each `Image=`/`Logo>` value in the manifest and confirming `Test-Path "Assets\<that filename>"` for every one of them. Don't rename files to look prettier — the manifest reference is the source of truth.Either way, before the first `winapp run`, sanity-check every asset reference in the manifest resolves:

```powershell
[xml]$m = Get-Content .\Package.appxmanifest
@($m.Package.Properties.Logo,
  $m.Package.Applications.Application.uap_VisualElements.Square150x150Logo,
  $m.Package.Applications.Application.uap_VisualElements.Square44x44Logo,
  $m.Package.Applications.Application.uap_VisualElements.uap_SplashScreen.Image,
  $m.Package.Applications.Application.uap_VisualElements.uap_DefaultTile.Wide310x150Logo
) | Where-Object { $_ } | ForEach-Object {
    if (-not (Test-Path $_)) { Write-Host "[MISSING] $_" -ForegroundColor Red }
}
```

### Manifest migration checklist (Windows.Desktop + runFullTrust)

The default UWP `Package.appxmanifest` declares itself as a Universal app, but a packaged WinUI 3 desktop app is a Win32
2 process with package identity — it needs a different shape. `winapp run` will register the AppX but **fail to deploy** 
 with `0x80073CF6` or "requires runFullTrust capability" if any of the following are missing.

When merging the UWP manifest into the scaffold's, make sure all of these are true:

1. **TargetDeviceFamily is `Windows.Desktop`** — not `Windows.Universal`:
   ```xml
   <Dependencies>
     <TargetDeviceFamily Name="Windows.Desktop" MinVersion="10.0.17763.0" MaxVersionTested="10.0.22621.0" />
   </Dependencies>
   ```
   Windows.Universal is UWP-only and rejected for a Win32 entrypoint.

2. **rescap namespace declared on `<Package>` and added to `IgnorableNamespaces`**:
   ```xml
   <Package
     xmlns="http://schemas.microsoft.com/appx/manifest/foundation/windows10"
     xmlns:uap="http://schemas.microsoft.com/appx/manifest/uap/windows10"
     xmlns:rescap="http://schemas.microsoft.com/appx/manifest/foundation/windows10/restrictedcapabilities"
     IgnorableNamespaces="uap rescap">
   ```
   Without the namespace declaration, item 3 below is silently stripped by AppX validation and you get the "requires run
nFullTrust" error at deploy time.

3. **`runFullTrust` capability declared**:
   ```xml
   <Capabilities>
     <rescap:Capability Name="runFullTrust" />
   </Capabilities>
   ```
   Packaged WinUI 3 desktop apps run outside the UWP AppContainer sandbox and must declare this. Keep any UWP `<Capabili
ity>` entries you actually use (e.g. `<DeviceCapability Name="webcam" />`) but add the `runFullTrust` line above no matte
er what.

4. **`<Application EntryPoint="$targetentrypoint$">`** — the WinUI 3 scaffold uses an MSBuild placeholder that the build
d resolves to the real entry point. Don't replace it with a literal `<UwpAppName>.App` (that's a UWP entry-point pattern)
).

### WUI analyzer warnings (UWP API residue)

The benchmark's `winapp build` injects the `Microsoft.WindowsAppSDK.Analyzers` package, which flags UWP-only APIs that c
compile cleanly under WinUI 3 but throw `COMException` at runtime — typically inside `Microsoft.UI.Xaml.Application.Start
t(...)` before any window can render. The runner sees this as `builds=true, runs=false`, and `Validate-UwpMigration.ps1` 
 will FAIL the build healthcheck for each unique warning.

| Rule | Symptom | Fix |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `WUI0002` | `Window.Current does not exist in WinUI 3 desktop apps` | Store the `Window` reference in `App.xaml.cs` (`
`App.Window`) and pass it where needed; see "Windowing" above. |
| `WUI0003` | `CoreDispatcher is UWP-only` | Use `DispatcherQueue.GetForCurrentThread()` and `TryEnqueue(...)`; see "Thr
reading" above. |
| `WUI0004` | `SystemNavigationManager.GetForCurrentView() is UWP-only` | Drop the system back button hookup, or use HWN
ND-based COM interop; see "GetForCurrentView Replacements" above. |

Treat every `warning WUI000\d` line in the build output as a defect — the analyzer does not produce false positives. Sea
arch for the API name in this document for the recommended replacement.

### Bootstrap-neutralized helper classes (filter-prone)

Some SDK Samples ship a shared `Common\NavigationHelper.cs` containing a `RootFrameNavigationHelper` class that wires AL
LT+Left / BrowserBack (virtual key 166) / mouse XButton1+XButton2 to frame back/forward navigation. Its WinUI 3 equivalen
nt requires `Microsoft.UI.Input.InputKeyboardSource.GetKeyState` or P/Invoke `GetAsyncKeyState` plus virtual-key code con
nstants — exactly the shape the model provider's content-safety filter classifies as "keylogger / surveillance" code. Mig
grating it routinely produces `CAPIError: 400 Output blocked by content filtering policy`.

`Initialize-UwpMigration.ps1` handles this automatically: before the agent ever sees the file, the bootstrap rewrites ju
ust the `RootFrameNavigationHelper` class body to a no-op stub (preserving the public ctor with `params object[] args` so
o callers compile no matter what signature they invoke). The audit log records `NEUTRALIZED  class RootFrameNavigationHel
lper`. The mapping row's Notes column is prefixed with `NEUTRALIZED:`.

**You don't need to migrate the neutralized class.** Just leave both it and the call site (typically `new RootFrameNavig
gationHelper(rootFrame)` in `App.xaml.cs`'s `OnLaunched`) untouched — back-nav is not a demonstrated feature of any UWP S
SDK sample. The remainder of `NavigationHelper.cs` (the `NavigationHelper` class used for per-page state save/restore, th
he LoadStateEventArgs / SaveStateEventArgs types) is migrated normally; it does not trigger the filter.

If you really want ALT+Left back-nav, add a single declarative XAML element to the AppBarButton that triggers GoBack:   

```xml
<AppBarButton Icon="Back" Click="OnBackClick">
  <AppBarButton.KeyboardAccelerators>
    <KeyboardAccelerator Key="Left" Modifiers="Menu"/>
  </AppBarButton.KeyboardAccelerators>
</AppBarButton>
```

`KeyboardAccelerator` is declarative and does not trip the filter.

i = 1;\n            foreach (Scenario s in scenarios)\n            {\n                itemCollection.Add(new Scenario { Title = $\"{i++}) {s.Title}\", ClassType = s.ClassType });\n            }\n    {\n                ScenarioControl.SelectedIndex = 0;\n            }\n        }\n\n        /// <summary>\n        /// Called whenever the user changes selection in the scenarios list.  This method will navigate to the respective\n        /// sample scenario page.\n        /// </summary>\n        /// <param name=\"sender\"></param>\n        /// <param name=\"e\"></param>\n        private void ScenarioControl_SelectionChanged(object sender, SelectionChangedEventArgs e)\n        {\n            // Clear the status block when navigating scenarios.\n            NotifyUser(String.Empty, NotifyType.StatusMessage);\n\n            ListBox scenarioListBox = sender as ListBox;\n            Scenario s = scenarioListBox.SelectedItem as Scenario;\n            if (s != null)\n            {\n                ScenarioFrame.Navigate(s.ClassType);\n                if (ActualWidth < 640)\n                {\n                    Splitter.IsPaneOpen = false;\n                }\n            }\n        }\n\n        public List<Scenario> Scenarios\n        {\n            get { return this.scenarios; Visibility.Visible : Visibility.Collapsed;\n            if (StatusBlock.Text != String.Empty)\n            {\n                StatusBorder.Visibility = Visibility.Visible;\n                StatusPanel.Visibility = Visibility.Visible;\n            }\n            else\n            {\n                StatusBorder.Visibility = Visibility.Collapsed;\n                StatusPanel.Visibility = Visibility.Collapsed;\n            }\n\n            // Raise an event if necessary to enable a screen reader to announce the status update.\n            var peer = FrameworkElementAutomationPeer.FromElement(StatusBlock);\n            if (peer != null)\n            {\n                peer.RaiseAutomationEvent(AutomationEvents.LiveRegionChanged);\n            }\n        }\n\n        async void Footer_Click(object sender, RoutedEventArgs e)\n        {\n            await Windows.System.Launcher.LaunchUriAsync(new Uri(((HyperlinkButton)sender).Tag.ToString()));\n        }\n\n        private void Button_Click(object sender, RoutedEventArgs e)\n        {\n            Splitter.IsPaneOpen = !Splitter.IsPaneOpen;\n        }\n    }\n\n    public enum NotifyType\n    {\n        StatusMessage,\n        ErrorMessage\n    };\n}","old_str":"using Microsoft.UI.Xaml.Controls;\n\n// To learn more about WinUI, the WinUI project structure,\n// and more about our project templates, see: http://aka.ms/winui-project-info.\n\nnamespace BackgroundMediaPlayback;\n\n/// <summary>\n/// The main content page displayed inside the application window.\n/// Add your UI logic, event handlers, and data binding here.\n/// </summary>\npublic sealed partial class MainPage : Page\n{\n    public MainPage()\n    {\n        InitializeComponent();\n\n        // TODO: Add your initialization logic here.\n    }\n}","path":"C:\\ado\\win-dev-skills-benchmark\\agent-benchmark\\results\\run32\\000_BackgroundMediaPlayback\\app\\BackgroundMediaPlayback\\MainPage.xaml.cs"}

60.         {
61.             ShowToast("Memory usage limit changing from " 
62.                 + (e.OldLimit / 1024) + "K to "
63.                 + (e.NewLimit / 1024) + "K");
64. 
65.             // If app memory usage is over the limit about to be enforced,
66.             // then reduce usage within 2 seconds to avoid suspending.
67.             if (MemoryManager.AppMemoryUsage >= e.NewLimit)
68.             {
69.                 ReduceMemoryUsage(e.NewLimit);
70.             }
71.         }
72. 
73.         /// <summary>
74.         /// Handle system notifications that the app has increased its
75.         /// memory usage level compared to its current target.
76.         /// </summary>
77.         /// <remarks>
78.         /// The app may have increased its usage or the app may have moved
79.         /// to the background and the system lowered the target the app
80.         /// is expected to reach. Either way, if the application wants
81.         /// to maintain its priority to avoid being suspended before
82.         /// other apps, it may need to reduce its memory usage.
83.         /// 
84.         /// This is not a replacement for handling AppMemoryUsageLimitChanging
85.         /// which is critical to ensure the app immediately gets below the new
86.         /// limit. However, once the app is allowed to continue running and
87.         /// policy is applied, some apps may wish to continue monitoring
88.         /// usage to ensure they remain below the limit.
89.         /// </remarks>
90.         /// <param name="sender"></param>
91.         /// <param name="e"></param>
92.         private void MemoryManager_AppMemoryUsageIncreased(object sender, object e)
93.         {
94.             ShowToast("Memory usage increased");
95. 
96.             // Obtain the current usage level
97.             var level = MemoryManager.AppMemoryUsageLevel;
98. 
99.             // Check the usage level to determine whether reducing memory is necessary.
100.             // Memory usage may have been fine when initially entering the background but
101.             // a short time later the app might be using more memory and need to trim back.
102.             if (level == AppMemoryUsageLevel.OverLimit || level == AppMemoryUsageLevel.High)
103.             {
104.                 ReduceMemoryUsage(MemoryManager.AppMemoryUsageLimit);
105.             }
106.         }
107.

155.         /// event, or receives a memory usage increased
156.         /// event it can optionally unload cached data or even its view content in 
157.         /// order to reduce memory usage and the chance of being suspended.
158.         /// 
159.         /// This must be called from multiple event handlers because an application may already
160.         /// be in a high memory usage state when entering the background or it
161.         /// may be in a low memory usage state with no need to unload resources yet
162.         /// and only enter a higher state later.
163.         /// </remarks>
164.         public void ReduceMemoryUsage(ulong limit)
165.         {
166.             // If the app has caches or other memory it can free, now is the time.
167.             // << App can release memory here >>
168. 
169.             // Additionally, if the application is currently
170.             // in background mode and still has a view with content
171.             // then the view can be released to save memory and 
172.             // can be recreated again later when leaving the background.
173.             if (isInBackgroundMode && MainWindow?.GetRootFrame()?.Content != null)
174.             {
175.                 ShowToast("Unloading view");
176. 
177.                 // Clear the view content. Note that views should rely on
178.                 // events like Page.Unloaded to further release resources. Be careful
179.                 // to also release event handlers in views since references can
180.                 // prevent objects from being collected.
181.                 MainWindow!.GetRootFrame().Content = null;
182. 
183.                 // Finally, clearing the content above and calling GC.Collect() below 
184.                 // is what will trigger each Page.Unloaded handler to be called.
185.                 // In order for the resources each page has allocated to be released,
186.                 // it is necessary that each Page also call GC.Collect() from its
187.                 // Page.Unloaded handler.
188.             }
189. 
190.             // Run the GC to collect released resources, including triggering
191.             // each Page.Unloaded handler to run.
192.             GC.Collect();
193. 
194.             ShowToast("Finished reducing memory usage");
195.         }
196. 
197.         /// <summary>
198.         /// Gets a string describing current memory usage
199.         /// </summary>
200.         /// <returns>String describing current memory usage</returns>
201.         private string GetMemoryUsageText()
202.         {
203.             return string.Format("[Memory: Level={0}, Usage={1}K, Target={2}K]", 
204.                 MemoryManager.AppMemoryUsageLevel, MemoryManager.AppMemoryUsage / 1024, MemoryManager.AppMemoryUsageLimit / 1024);
205.         }
206. 
207.         /// <summary>
208.         /// Sends a toast notification
209.         /// </summary>
210.         /// <param name="msg">Message to send</param>
211.         /// <param name="subMsg">Sub message</param>
212.         public void ShowToast(string msg, string subMsg = null)
213.         {
214.             if (subMsg == null)
215.                 subMsg = GetMemoryUsageText();
216. 
217.             Debug.WriteLine(msg + "\n" + subMsg);
218. 
219.             if (!SettingsService.Instance.ToastOnAppEvents)
220.                 return;

## Summary

The BackgroundMediaPlayback UWP app has been successfully migrated to WinUI 3 / Windows App SDK 2.2. Here's what was done:

**Code migrations:**
- **Windowing** (TODOs 001–006): Replaced `Window.Current` with a static `App.MainWindow` property; `MainWindow.GetRootFrame()` exposes the navigation frame
- **Threading** (TODOs 009–020): Replaced `CoreDispatcher`/`RunAsync` with `DispatcherQueue.TryEnqueue` across all ViewModels and Scenario1
- **Notifications** (TODOs 007–008): Replaced `ToastNotificationManager` with `AppNotificationManager` from Microsoft.Windows.AppNotifications
- **PopupMenu** → `MenuFlyout` (Scenario1 speed selector)
- **SettingsService**: `RoamingSettings` → `LocalSettings` (roaming removed in WinAppSDK)

**Structure additions:**
- Created `Scenario1.xaml`, `Settings.xaml`, `Views/PlaylistView.xaml`, `Styles/Styles.xaml`, and a WinUI 3 `MainPage.xaml/cs` from the UWP SharedContent
- Updated `Package.appxmanifest` for Windows.Desktop + `runFullTrust`
- Copied playlist.json and media assets (Ring01–Ring03 audio/images)

**WinAppSDK 2.2 adaptations:**
- `Application.EnteredBackground/LeavingBackground/Suspending/Resuming` don't exist in WinAppSDK 2.2 desktop — removed those lifecycle hooks; `MemoryManager` pressure callbacks still fire for packaged apps
